<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-sewing** [claim](#stmt-inp-sewing) / [proof](#stmt-inp-sewing): Cite the discrete encoding and the peanosphere theorems -> Contour coupling of the map with the quantum cone
- **step-topology** [claim](#stmt-inp-topology) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-topology): Uniform joining from fiber generators, planar separation, degree argument -> Projections with path joining and winding comparison
- **step-complete-core** [claim](#stmt-inp-complete-core) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-complete-core): Select runs near the compact set, exact band links with exact caps, positive slack -> Exact local graph pieces from finite prescriptions
- **step-sandwich** [claim](#stmt-inp-sandwich) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-sandwich): Cover by relative-time intervals with rational value constraints -> Selector sandwich for vertices and values
- **step-kernels** [claim](#stmt-inp-kernel-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-kernel-transfer): Exact density of starts, local limit theorem, bridge tightness, disintegration -> Conditional product law of numerical outputs
- **step-local-kernels** [claim](#stmt-inp-local-kernels) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-local-kernels): Read block inputs from local fields; Poisson mapping theorem for marks -> Field-local kernels
- **step-transport** [claim](#stmt-inp-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-transport): Track block inputs under field shift and conformal change -> Unit and coordinate transport of kernels
- **step-gaussian** [claim](#stmt-inp-gaussian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-gaussian): Cameron-Martin and domain comparison; density truncation -> Local equivalence with Gaussian fields
- **step-reflection** [claim](#stmt-inp-reflection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-reflection): Exchange coordinates discretely; reflect with negated imaginary field in the continuum -> Primal-dual symmetry
- **step-array-interface** [claim](#stmt-inp-array-interface) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inp-array-interface): Compactness of outputs and diagonal extraction -> Joint local-array extraction
- **step-el-traffic** [claim](#stmt-nd-el-traffic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nd-el-traffic): Nearest point of the convex hull of path vectors; Cauchy-Schwarz -> Extremal length equals minimal traffic
- **step-germs** [claim](#stmt-nd-germs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nd-germs): Triviality of Gaussian germ field via zero capacity of a point; conditional independence -> Deterministic point germs
- **step-coverage** [claim](#stmt-nd-coverage) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nd-coverage): Apply annulus lemma to local kernels; Borel-Cantelli -> Many aligned annular barriers
- **step-densities** [claim](#stmt-nd-densities) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nd-densities): Sum shell densities, Cauchy-Schwarz across radii, level crossing count -> Displacement-charging densities
- **step-nondegeneration** [claim](#stmt-nd-nondegeneration) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nd-nondegeneration): Contradiction: small-traffic flows, rectifiable limit, germ determinism, transverse crossing pairing -> Positive lower limit of quantiles
- **step-ring-cutoffs** [claim](#stmt-nd-cutoffs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nd-cutoffs): Densities in unit energy scale; clipped distance construction -> Bounded-energy ring cutoffs
- **step-circuit** [claim](#stmt-en-circuit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-circuit): Coarea on level sets of a dual cutoff, topological separation -> Separating circuits with controlled variation
- **step-harmonic-compactness** [claim](#stmt-en-harmonic-compactness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-harmonic-compactness): Stack of annuli, oscillation on circuits, maximum principle, Arzela-Ascoli -> Equicontinuity and compactness of harmonic functions
- **step-gluing** [claim](#stmt-en-fundamental) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-fundamental): Difference identity and convexity -> Gluing estimate with bounded-energy weights
- **step-envelope** [claim](#stmt-en-envelope) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-envelope): Insert an intermediate selector test between two tolerance levels -> Relaxed energy with recovery sequences
- **step-measure** [claim](#stmt-en-measure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-measure): Parallelogram identity, subadditivity by gluing, metric outer measure, smooth upper bound -> Radon energy measures
- **step-representation** [claim](#stmt-en-representation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-representation): Density of linear-function measures; coercivity from circuits at grid nodes -> Matrix-field representation
- **step-scalar** [claim](#stmt-en-scalar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-scalar): Determinism of germs and covariance under rotations and reflection -> Matrix field equals a scalar multiple of the identity
- **step-condenser** [claim](#stmt-en-condenser) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-condenser): Upper bound by recovery; compactness and boundary continuity; lower bound -> Condenser energy converges to the scalar times the capacity
- **step-current-period** [claim](#stmt-en-current-period) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-current-period): Rotate the current to dual edges; integrate along filled loops; compactness of branches -> Dual potential with period controlled by condenser energy; scalar at most one
- **step-recovery-measures** [claim](#stmt-en-recovery-measures) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-recovery-measures): Subsequential vague limits and Portmanteau comparison -> Energy measures of recoveries converge
- **step-angular-recovery** [claim](#stmt-en-angular-recovery) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-angular-recovery): Glue recoveries of angle branches with a partition of unity; add a canonical angular cochain -> Exact unit-period cochains with controlled energy
- **step-reciprocity** [claim](#stmt-en-reciprocity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-reciprocity): Pair the current with the unit-period cochain and apply Cauchy-Schwarz -> Scalar equals one
- **step-variational** [claim](#stmt-en-variational) / [proof](#proof-stmt-en-variational): Combine representation, scalar and normalization -> Uniform conformal energy limit
- **step-block-density** [claim](#stmt-sph-block-density) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sph-block-density): Convergence of excursion density with second-moment truncation -> Density tilt under excursion conditioning
- **step-sphere-transfer** [claim](#stmt-sph-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sph-transfer): Enlarge arrays, extract jointly with the free block, apply the planar identity and absolute continuity -> Energy identification on interval surfaces
- **step-removability** [claim](#stmt-sph-removability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sph-removability): Logarithmic cutoffs show zero capacity of a point -> Sobolev removability of the tour endpoint
- **step-sphere-energy** [claim](#stmt-sph-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sph-energy): Exhaust the complement of the endpoint, glue local recoveries, annuli around the endpoint -> Global sphere energy statements
- **step-comparison** [claim](#stmt-sph-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sph-comparison): Contour correspondence with vanishing distortion; occupation measure of tour starts -> Metric, density and measure convergence in the same coupling
- **step-forest** [claim](#stmt-gr-forest) / [proof](#proof-stmt-gr-forest): Matrix-tree theorem, rank-one determinant lemma, Cauchy-Binet over two-component forests -> Spanning-forest formula for the centered inverse Laplacian
- **step-tour-cut** [claim](#stmt-gr-tour-cut) / [proof](#proof-stmt-gr-tour-cut): Count departing steps; parity argument on dual contour -> Cut mass and cut size in the tour
- **step-dyck** [claim](#stmt-gr-dyck) / [proof](#proof-stmt-gr-dyck): Rerooting bijection, killed-walk kernel, dyadic chaining -> Distance density, moment and diameter bounds
- **step-one-scale** [claim](#stmt-gr-one-scale) / [proof](#proof-stmt-gr-one-scale): Balanced schedules, vertical contour bound, nested cuts counted by a contour diameter -> Uniform second moment per scale
- **step-green-bound** [claim](#stmt-gr-green-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-gr-one-scale-2): Grid reduction over dyadic scales and geometric summation -> Small-mass Green estimate
- **step-smooth-core** [claim](#stmt-dyn-smooth-core) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dyn-smooth-core): Mollification and partition of unity -> Continuous Sobolev functions lie in the form domain
- **step-resolvent** [claim](#stmt-dyn-resolvent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dyn-resolvent): Subtract a Green potential, apply harmonic compactness, identify minimizer of the resolvent functional -> Uniform resolvent convergence
- **step-feller** [claim](#stmt-dyn-feller) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dyn-feller): Spectral argument, trace on circles, weak-form maximum principle -> Uniform resolvent approximation of identity
- **step-tightness** [claim](#stmt-dyn-tightness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dyn-tightness): Bounded-generator test functions, optional stopping, Aldous criterion -> Uniform exit estimate and tightness
- **step-deterministic-criterion** [claim](#stmt-dyn-conditional) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dyn-tightness-2): Laplace transforms of finite-dimensional distributions identify every subsequential limit -> Path-law convergence on deterministic graph sequences
- **step-energy-clock** [claim](#stmt-dyn-energy-clock) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dyn-energy-clock): Direct computation with the degree measure -> Generator energy identity
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-dyn-energy-clock-2): Skorokhod coupling of all extracted variables, deterministic criterion on each realization, correspondence gluing into one metric space, subsequence principle -> Convergence of conditional path laws with clock multiplier one

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-energy-clock-identity**: For every finite connected multigraph with n edges, the accelerated half-edge walk generator satisfies an exact identity: minus the stationary-measure pairing of u with n times the generator applied to u equals one half of the unnormalized network energy of u, with loops contributing zero.
- **inv-loop-degree-convention**: Each loop contributes two to the degree, so loops add half-edges at their vertex yet make no displacement and add nothing to the energy.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-nonuniform-walk**: Apply the exact one-half energy identity to a walk whose steps are not uniform over incident half-edges, for instance with weighted conductances. -> The generator is then not the degree-normalized half-edge average, so the relation between the clock and the unit-conductance energy no longer holds.
- **anti-loop-displacement**: Treat a loop as an ordinary displacement of the walker when estimating motion or energy. -> Loop steps change neither position nor energy, so counting them as moves overstates displacement.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The generator-energy identity is a finite algebraic statement on explicit multigraphs. It can be evaluated exactly in rational arithmetic and compared with an independently computed energy.

```python
# INPUTS: stdin text: 'V E', then V integer values of u, then E lines 'a b' of vertex pairs (0-based, loops and repeats allowed).
# PRECONDITIONS: the multigraph is connected, V >= 1, E >= 1, so every vertex has positive degree.
# OUTPUTS: one exact rational number: minus the stationary pairing of u with E times the half-edge walk generator applied to u.
# TIME: O(V + E) fraction operations.
# SPACE: O(V + E).
# ARITHMETIC: exact Fraction arithmetic, no floating point.
# FAILURE: a vertex of degree zero raises ZeroDivisionError, which is a nonzero exit.
import sys
from fractions import Fraction

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.read().split()
    V = int(data[0])
    E = int(data[1])
    u = [int(x) for x in data[2:2 + V]]
    edges = [(int(data[2 + V + 2 * i]), int(data[3 + V + 2 * i])) for i in range(E)]
    inc = [[] for _ in range(V)]
    for a, b in edges:
        inc[a].append(b)
        inc[b].append(a)
    total=Fraction(0)
    for v in range(V):
        deg = len(inc[v])
        mu = Fraction(deg, 2 * E)
        Lu = sum(Fraction(u[w]-u[v]) for w in inc[v])/deg
        total -= mu*u[v]*E*Lu
    print(total)

main()
```

        "observed_exit": 1,

      },
      {
        "expected_failure": "Killed by the triangle test vector: output differs from the expected value.",
        "id": "mut-wrong-normaliser",
        "mutation": "The half-edge average divides by the edge count instead of the degree.",
        "observed_exit": 1,
        "receipt_sha256": "44cbedb3447094d8901593ebf7bf239b6742e1060996c40590d7a9ba1c851569"
      },
      {
        "expected_failure": "Killed by the first test vector: output is shifted.",
        "id": "mut-bad-start",
        "mutation": "The accumulator starts from a nonzero value.",
        "observed_exit": 1,
        "receipt_sha256": "582638573f58803602d1e7401afc9829ea75671f9b83d8ac672952164e3bfbb1"
      }
    ],
    "oracle_code": "import sys\nfrom fractions import Fraction\n\ndef main():\n    inp = open(sys.argv[1]).read().split()\n    out = open(sys.argv[2]).read().strip()\n    exp = Fraction(sys.argv[3].strip())\n    V = int(inp[0])\n    E = int(inp[1])\n    u = [Fraction(int(x)) for x in inp[2:2 + V]]\n    H = [[Fraction(0)] * V for _ in range(V)]\n    for i in range(E):\n        a = int(inp[2 + V + 2 * i])\n        b = int(inp[3 + V + 2 * i])\n        if a == b:\n            continue\n        H[a][a] += 1\n        H[b][b] += 1\n        H[a][b] -= 1\n        H[b][a] -= 1\n    q = Fraction(0)\n    for i in range(V):\n        for j in range(V):\n            q += u[i] * H[i][j] * u[j]\n    half = q / 2\n    try:\n        got = Fraction(out)\n    except Exception:\n        sys.exit(1)\n    sys.exit(0 if (got == half and half == exp) else 1)\n\nmain()\n",
    "output_contract": "A single exact rational number equal to one half of the sum over edges of squared value differences (loops contribute zero).",
    "reason": "The generator-energy identity is a finite algebraic statement on explicit multigraphs. It can be evaluated exactly in rational arithmetic and compared with an independently computed energy.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: stdin text: 'V E', then V integer values of u, then E lines 'a b' of vertex pairs (0-based, loops and repeats allowed).\n# PRECONDITIONS: the multigraph is connected, V >= 1, E >= 1, so every vertex has positive degree.\n# OUTPUTS: one exact rational number: minus the stationary pairing of u with E times the half-edge walk generator applied to u.\n# TIME: O(V + E) fraction operations.\n# SPACE: O(V + E).\n# ARITHMETIC: exact Fraction arithmetic, no floating point.\n# FAILURE: a vertex of degree zero raises ZeroDivisionError, which is a nonzero exit.\nimport sys\nfrom fractions import Fraction\n\ndef main():\n    data = sys.stdin.read().split()\n    V = int(data[0])\n    E = int(data[1])\n    u = [int(x) for x in data[2:2 + V]]\n    edges = [(int(data[2 + V + 2 * i]), int(data[3 + V + 2 * i])) for i in range(E)]\n    inc = [[] for _ in range(V)]\n    for a, b in edges:\n        inc[a].append(b)\n        inc[b].append(a)\n    total=Fraction(0)\n    for v in range(V):\n        deg = len(inc[v])\n        mu = Fraction(deg, 2 * E)\n        Lu = sum(Fraction(u[w]-u[v]) for w in inc[v])/deg\n        total -= mu*u[v]*E*Lu\n    print(total)\n\nmain()\n",
    "test_vectors": [
      {
        "expected": "1/2",
        "input": "2 1\n0 1\n0 1\n"
      },

        "input": "3 3\n0 1 3\n0 1\n1 2\n0 2\n"
      },

        "input": "1 1\n5\n0 0\n"
      },

      },

        "input": "3 3\n1 0 2\n0 1\n1 2\n1 1\n"
      },
      {

      }
    ]

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a probabilistic scaling-limit theorem; the proof gives no finite procedure that could be shipped as a library routine. |
| games_simulation | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The model fixes a precise simulation recipe: a rate-one Poisson walk choosing incident half-edges uniformly, started from the degree measure. The convergence statement only calibrates its clock, so it is adaptable rather than a ready simulator. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No tool or checker is specified; only an asymptotic identification of a diffusion limit is proved. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves existence of a limit and exact energy identities; no algorithm with stated cost is given. The spanning-forest formula is an expectation identity, not a procedure. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Only the combinatorial encoding of maps by a two-coordinate walk is used, as a proof device rather than a data structure with operations. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the statements concerns parallel architectures or hardware cost. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, adversary or security notion appears. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem is posed; graph energy and Green bounds are analytic tools for a diffusion limit. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The variational energies are used to identify a conductivity constant, not to solve an optimisation problem with a guarantee on a given instance. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No communication, failure or consensus model is treated, although the walk is a random process on a graph. |
| other | True | moderate | inspirational | stated | The result serves mathematical physics of random surfaces: it fixes the clock of Liouville Brownian motion as the scaling limit of walks on tree-weighted planar maps. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-planar-tree-ensemble** (hypothesis): The theorem is proved only for the ensemble in which a rooted planar map with n edges carries a uniformly chosen spanning tree, so that the map marginal is weighted by its number of spanning trees. Other planar ensembles are not covered. Blocked use: ['Applying the clock multiplier one to uniform planar maps without the tree weight', 'Applying the result to non-planar or directed graphs']
- **lim-stationary-start** (hypothesis): The walk starts from the degree measure and chooses incident half-edges uniformly at the times of a rate-one Poisson clock. The clock multiplier one is tied to this start and these jump rates. Blocked use: ['Starting the walk from a fixed vertex and quoting the same convergence', 'Using weighted conductances with the same clock constant']
- **lim-spatial-normalization** (hypothesis): Convergence holds only after the deterministic spatial rescaling by the factor defined through the centering of the log-diameter, and relies on the metric-measure limit imported from the companion work. Blocked use: ['Reading the result for the unscaled graph metric', 'Using the theorem without the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov premise']
- **lim-distributional-convergence** (formal_scope): The conclusion is convergence in distribution of the random quadruple for the path-law metric, obtained through subsequences and a Skorokhod coupling. It gives no rate and no statement for a specific finite n. Blocked use: ['Quoting an error bound at a given finite map size', 'Treating the convergence as almost sure on the original probability space']
- **lim-nonconstructive-extraction** (nonconstructive): The joint extraction of numerical arrays and the field comparisons rest on compactness and diagonal subsequences, so no finite procedure produces them. Blocked use: ['Presenting the extraction as an algorithm', 'Deriving computable constants from the extraction step']
- **lim-constants-unspecified** (approximation): The Green bound and the ring-cutoff energy bound carry constants that are not computed; the Green bound has a random prefactor controlled only in expectation. Blocked use: ['Using the Green bound as a quantitative estimate with explicit constants', 'Using the cutoff energy bound as a numerical value']
- **lim-ordinary-charts-marked-point** (edge_case): Local energy identification holds on ordinary patches away from the cone point and, on the sphere, away from the tour endpoint; the endpoint is handled afterwards by removability for continuous Sobolev functions. Blocked use: ['Claiming local results at the marked point without the removability argument', 'Applying the energy limit to discontinuous targets']
- **lim-deterministic-criterion** (hypothesis): The deterministic path-law criterion needs all four hypotheses together: weak convergence of measures with vanishing edge length, the energy lower bound with recovery, harmonic equicontinuity, and the Green bound. Blocked use: ['Using the criterion with only energy convergence', 'Applying it to measures with atoms or without full support']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that stationary random walk on a planar map sampled with weight equal to its number of spanning trees converges to Liouville Brownian motion on the unit-area $`\sqrt2`$-Liouville quantum sphere. The convergence retains the conditional path law jointly with the measured metric space. The walk chooses uniformly among all incident half-edges and starts from the stationary degree measure. For total attempt rate one and the continuum Dirichlet form with factor 1/2, the time acceleration is exactly the number of map edges. The result uses the companion contour and metric limits for this same ensemble.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A scaling limit for the metric and area of a random surface does not by itself determine the motion of a particle on that surface. The electrical conductances of the approximating graphs can change the limiting diffusion, even when their measured metric spaces converge. A further question is whether the time needed to see the continuum motion has a single deterministic normalization.

We resolve this question for planar maps weighted by their numbers of spanning trees. The geometric input is the metric-measure limit and the contour reconstruction theory of <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]. We prove the additional convergence of the conditional walk laws, with an exact linear clock. The proof separates the identification of the electrical energy from the control of the speed measure. This separation is useful because neither part follows from metric-measure convergence alone.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The model and the continuum process

A rooted planar map is a finite connected graph embedded in the oriented sphere, up to orientation-preserving homeomorphism, with a distinguished oriented edge. Loops and multiple edges are allowed. For $n\ge1$, let $(M_n,T_n)$ be uniform among rooted maps with $n$ edges decorated by one of their spanning trees. Thus the marginal weight of $M_n$ is proportional to its number of spanning trees; conditional on $M_n$, the tree $T_n$ is uniform. Write $V_n$ for the vertex set and $d_n$ for graph distance using all map edges, each of length one. Set <a id="eq:degree-measure"></a>$$\label{eq:degree-measure}
 \mu_n=\sum_{v\in V_n}\frac{\deg(v)}{2n}\,\delta_v,$$ where each loop contributes two to the degree.

<a id="eq:walk-generator"></a>
Conditional on the map, let $X^n$ start from $\mu_n$. At the times of an independent rate-one Poisson process it chooses an incident half-edge uniformly and moves to its other endpoint. Choosing a loop makes no displacement. Its generator is $$\label{eq:walk-generator}
 (L_nf)(v)=\frac1{\deg(v)}
   \sum_{e\text{ a half-edge at }v}
       \bigl(f(\operatorname{other}(e))-f(v)\bigr).$$ The measure $\mu_n$ is stationary and reversible for this walk.

<a id="eq:continuum-form"></a>
Let $(S,h)$ be an ordinary unit-area $\sqrt2$-Liouville quantum sphere, with its distinguished points forgotten. We use the ordinary fixed-area sphere law of Duplantier, Miller, and Sheffield <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[DMS](#ref-DMS)]. Its quantum area measure $\mu_h$ has total mass one. Write $D_h$ for its Liouville quantum gravity metric in a fixed deterministic normalization. Identify the underlying conformal sphere with the round sphere. The continuum process $B^h$ is the conservative $\mu_h$-symmetric diffusion associated with the closure on $L^2(\mu_h)$ of $$\label{eq:continuum-form}
 \mathcal E_h(f,g)=\frac12\int_{S^2}
      \langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle_{\mathrm{round}}
                    \,\mathrm d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_{\mathrm{round}},
 \qquad f,g\in C^\infty(S^2).$$ The closed form uses quasi-continuous representatives. This is Liouville Brownian motion, started with conditional law $\mu_h$; see <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[GRV](#ref-GRV), [Berestycki](#ref-Berestycki)] and the Dirichlet-form framework in <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[FOT](#ref-FOT)]. Conformal invariance of the two-dimensional Dirichlet integral makes <a href="#eq:continuum-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-form">[eq:continuum-form]</a> independent of the conformal coordinate. Its factor $1/2$ fixes the continuum clock.

<a id="eq:spatial-normalization"></a>
<a id="eq:metric-premise"></a>
For precision we fix the spatial normalization used throughout. For a finite real random variable $Y$, let $m(Y)$ be the unique real solution of $\mathbb E[\arctan(Y-m(Y))]=0$. Put $$\label{eq:spatial-normalization}
 \Delta_n=\max\{1,\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(V_n,d_n)\},\qquad
 \Delta=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(S,D_h),\qquad
 a_n=\exp\{m(\log\Delta)-m(\log\Delta_n)\}.$$ Both centers use unconditional laws, so $a_n$ is deterministic. We use the geometric limit of <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)] in the form $$\label{eq:metric-premise}
 a_n\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad (V_n,a_nd_n,\mu_n)
       \xrightarrow{\ d\ }(S,D_h,\mu_h)
 \quad\text{in Gromov--Hausdorff--Prokhorov topology}.$$ The contour and topological results from that work needed in addition to <a href="#eq:metric-premise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:metric-premise">[eq:metric-premise]</a> are stated in Section <a href="#sec:inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inputs">2</a>.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Retaining the conditional path law

Fix $T>0$. If $(K,d,\mu)$ is a compact metric probability space, let $Q$ be a Borel probability measure on $D([0,T],K)$. We regard $(K,d,\mu,Q)$ up to isometries transporting both measures. For two such quadruples, define $\delta_T$ as the infimum over isometric embeddings $i:K\to Z$ and $j:K'\to Z$ into a common complete separable metric space of <a id="eq:delta"></a>$$\label{eq:delta}
 \max\left\{
 d_H^Z(iK,jK'),\ d_P^Z(i_*\mu,j_*\mu'),\
 d_P^{D([0,T],Z)}(i_*Q,j_*Q')\right\}.$$ Here $d_H$ and $d_P$ denote Hausdorff and Prokhorov distances. The last term uses the Skorokhod $J_1$ topology: time changes are increasing continuous bijections of $[0,T]$, and both their uniform time displacement and the uniform spatial discrepancy tend to zero. Each of the three distances may be truncated at one. An embedding acts on a path at every time.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (Linear clock and conditional laws). *For every finite $T>0$, define $$Q_n^T=\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}\bigl((X^n_{nt})_{0\le t\le T}\mid M_n,T_n\bigr),
 \qquad
 Q_h^T=\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}\bigl((B^h_t)_{0\le t\le T}\mid h\bigr).$$ With the spatial normalization <a href="#eq:spatial-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-normalization">[eq:spatial-normalization]</a>, $$(V_n,a_nd_n,\mu_n,Q_n^T)
       \xrightarrow{\ d\ }(S,D_h,\mu_h,Q_h^T)
       \qquad\text{for }\delta_T,$$ through all positive integers $n$. Thus the deterministic clock multiplier for <a href="#eq:walk-generator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:walk-generator">[eq:walk-generator]</a> and <a href="#eq:continuum-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-form">[eq:continuum-form]</a> is $c=1$.*

</div>

</div>

The random probability law is part of the state in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. For example, the same argument gives joint convergence of any fixed number of conditionally independent stationary walks on the same map: in a common embedding, the associated conditional joint laws are the finite products of $Q_n^T$. Their weak convergence follows from that of $Q_n^T$. This preserves information about the shared environment.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Context and related work

Mullin’s enumeration of tree-rooted maps <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Mullin](#ref-Mullin)] gives the combinatorial starting point: the selected spanning tree and its complementary dual tree encode a map by a two-coordinate walk. The continuum counterpart is the mating-of-trees construction of Duplantier, Miller, and Sheffield <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DMS](#ref-DMS)]. At $\gamma=\sqrt2$ its boundary-length coordinates are independent Brownian motions. Miller and Sheffield <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[MSSphere](#ref-MSSphere)] give the corresponding finite-area sphere description, and their imaginary-geometry construction <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[IG4](#ref-IG4)] supplies the space-filling $\mathrm{SLE}_8$ between the two continuum trees. Couplings of discrete and continuum encodings have also been used to compare graph distances across random-map models <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[GHS](#ref-GHS)]. The continuum metric itself is characterized in <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[GMMetric](#ref-GMMetric)], with conformal covariance established in <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GMConformal](#ref-GMConformal)].

Liouville Brownian motion was constructed by Garban, Rhodes, and Vargas <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GRV](#ref-GRV)] and independently by Berestycki <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Berestycki](#ref-Berestycki)] through a change of Brownian time determined by quantum area. Its Dirichlet-form description <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GRVForm](#ref-GRVForm)] separates two structures: the energy is conformally invariant, while the speed measure is quantum area. This distinction explains why the clock is a separate part of a random-map scaling limit.

Several works establish important parts of the discrete picture. Gwynne, Miller, and Sheffield prove convergence of the Tutte embedding of mated-CRT maps, including conditional walk-law convergence modulo time parameterization <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GMS](#ref-GMS)]. Their estimates for harmonic functions compare discrete and continuum energies up to constants and control oscillation <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GMSHarmonic](#ref-GMSHarmonic)]; their invariance principle for ergodic scale-free environments develops a broader framework for embedded networks <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[GMS2](#ref-GMS2)]. Gwynne and Miller obtain resistance, return-probability, and displacement estimates for several infinite random planar maps, including spanning-tree-weighted maps <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[GM](#ref-GM)].

Berestycki and Gwynne prove parameterized, quenched convergence to Liouville Brownian motion for mated-CRT maps <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[BG](#ref-BG)]. Their deterministic time scale is an annealed exit-time median, comparable to the inverse quantum area of a cell. Whether its ratio to that inverse area converges is left open in <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BG](#ref-BG), Remark 1.3]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> concerns the finite combinatorial tree-weighted map and its stationary half-edge walk. It retains the conditional path law together with metric and area, and identifies the acceleration as exactly $n$ for <a href="#eq:continuum-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuum-form">[eq:continuum-form]</a>.

Our geometric inputs are the companion results in <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]. The nondegeneration argument adapts the local-flow method in <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[ConformalMaps](#ref-ConformalMaps), Section ``The canonical conformal structure''] to a primal and a dual electrical network. The use of planar duality to determine an effective conductivity has classical precedents, including Dykhne’s exact-conductivity argument <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Dykhne](#ref-Dykhne)]. Here we must first construct the energy limit without regularity assumptions on the shapes of discrete faces. A separate Green estimate in the original finite-map law then controls sources of small stationary mass and supplies the missing clock information.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Proof strategy and reusable estimates

For a finite graph embedded in the sphere, write <a id="eq:network-energy"></a>$$\label{eq:network-energy}
 \mathsf e(u)=\sum_{ab\in E}(u(a)-u(b))^2,$$ with one term for each unoriented edge, including multiplicities. Loops contribute zero. The same convention defines $\mathsf e^{*}$ on the dual graph. The two main tasks are to identify the limit of these unnormalized energies and to pass from that limit to motion in actual time.

The contour description of the map gives local finite graph pieces in conformal coordinates. Section <a href="#sec:inputs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inputs">2</a> explains why the local reconstruction and conditional-kernel calculations of <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)] also apply to extremal lengths and constrained energy minima on these pieces. Exact edge incidences matter here: approximation of graph distances alone would not identify an energy. The extension retains compact numerical outputs before extracting a subsequence, and uses a change of measure depending only on the contour inputs.

Section <a href="#sec:nondegeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nondegeneration">3</a> proves bounded-energy cutoff estimates without geometric control of individual faces. Its central argument is a planar obstruction to simultaneous degeneracy of the primal and dual networks. If annular extremal lengths could vanish, low-energy flows would have a nonzero limiting average length measure. Locality and triviality of field point germs produce transverse primal and dual crossing flows at a typical point. Every such pair crosses an edge and its dual, contradicting the vanishing traffic norms. The cutoffs then yield harmonic compactness.

Section <a href="#sec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy">4</a> identifies every local variational limit as $\lambda\int|\nabla u|^2$, with the same positive deterministic $\lambda$ for the primal and dual. To determine $\lambda$, we compare a condenser on an annulus with the period of its rotated current. The comparison in one direction gives $\lambda\le1$; recovery of an angular cochain and the signed primal–dual pairing give $\lambda\ge1$. Thus the coefficient is exactly one in every subsequence. Section <a href="#sec:sphere" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sphere">5</a> transfers the result to fixed-area spheres using interior excursion densities and removes the single omitted contour endpoint by zero Sobolev capacity.

<a id="eq:intro-green"></a>
The remaining speed-measure issue is addressed by the following estimate, proved in Section <a href="#sec:green" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:green">6</a>. Let $H_n$ be the positive unweighted graph Laplacian. For $\mu_n(f)=0$, define $G_nf$ by $H_nG_nf=\mu_nf$ and $\mu_n(G_nf)=0$, where the product $\mu_nf$ on the right denotes its vector of vertex masses. For every $0<\rho<1/2$ there are map-measurable random variables $A_n$ with $\sup_n\mathbb EA_n<\infty$ such that, simultaneously for $\|f\|_\infty\le1$, $$\label{eq:intro-green}
 \|G_nf\|_\infty\le A_n\,\mu_n(|f|)^\rho.$$ Its proof is entirely in the fixed-size map law. A spanning-forest identity expresses the centered inverse Laplacian through tree cuts; the contour bijection expresses each cut size as one plus a dual-tree distance. Dyck-excursion estimates then control the interval sums. This estimate can be used independently of the energy identification.

Finally, Section <a href="#sec:dynamics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:dynamics">7</a> uses <a href="#eq:intro-green" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-green">[eq:intro-green]</a> to obtain uniform resolvent convergence and a stopping-time tightness estimate. The resolvents identify the conditional path laws by their integrated finite-dimensional distributions. The normalization is visible already at the discrete level: $$-\langle f,nL_nf\rangle_{L^2(\mu_n)}=\tfrac12\mathsf e(f).$$ Combining this identity with the coefficient $\lambda=1$ gives the clock in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:inputs"></a>
### Sewing and local electrical observations

Electrical energy depends on every edge, including parallel edges. Consequently, a description that approximates graph distances does not by itself suffice for our purpose. This section explains how the local sewing construction of <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)] retains exact graph pieces and gives conditional laws for numerical observations on those pieces. The observations may be extremal lengths or constrained energy minima; we do not yet assert that they are bounded or identify their limits. The construction has three parts. We recover complete finite graph pieces from contour cells, pass their numerical observations to conditional limiting laws, and then read those laws from the fields in the corresponding spatial regions.

Throughout this section, $$\gamma=\sqrt2,\qquad Q=\frac2\gamma+\frac\gamma2=\frac3{\sqrt2},
 \qquad \chi=\frac1{\sqrt2}.$$ All quantum surfaces use the field and boundary-length normalizations of <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]. A compact containment $K\Subset U$ means that $K$ is compact and contained in the open coordinate domain $U$. A buffer for $K$ is an open neighborhood whose closure is compactly contained in $U$.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The discrete graph and its continuum background

Let $Z^{\mathrm d}=(L^{\mathrm d},R^{\mathrm d})$ be a bilateral walk whose increments are independent and uniform on the four axial unit vectors. For integer times $i\le j$, set <a id="inp:vertex-equivalence"></a>$$\label{inp:vertex-equivalence}
 i\sim_L j
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 L^{\mathrm d}_i=L^{\mathrm d}_j
       =\min_{i\le k\le j}L^{\mathrm d}_k.$$ The equivalence classes are the primal vertices. Horizontal steps give tree edges between consecutive classes, with the two traversals of a tree edge counted as one edge. A vertical up-step ending at $u$ and the vertical down-step ending at $v>u$ are matched when <a id="inp:step-match"></a>$$\label{inp:step-match}
 R^{\mathrm d}_{u-1}=R^{\mathrm d}_v
      <\min_{u\le k<v}R^{\mathrm d}_k.$$ Each matched pair contributes one further edge between $[u-1]_L$ and $[v-1]_L$. Distinct matched pairs remain distinct edges when their endpoints agree. In particular loops and parallel edges are retained. Interchanging $L^{\mathrm d}$ and $R^{\mathrm d}$ gives the dual graph and its complementary spanning tree.

For a finite interval of indices, the *internal graph* uses the same vertex relation, all horizontal steps in that interval, and the vertical pairs whose two occurrences lie in the interval. Its vertices embed injectively into the full graph: the minimum test between two retained times is the same in both constructions. It need not contain every full-graph edge between those vertices.

<a id="stmt-inp-sewing"></a>
<div id="inp:sewing" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2** (Sewing and continuum background). *The following couplings and normalizations will be used.*

1.  *A uniform rooted map–spanning-tree pair with $n$ edges is encoded by the axial walk conditioned to remain in $\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^2$ and return to zero after $2n$ steps. The preceding rules recover every primal and dual edge with its incidences.*

2.  *Give the bilateral walk an independent Poisson clock of rate $m$ and multiply its heights by $m^{-1/2}$. As $m\to\infty$, its interpolated contour converges locally uniformly in distribution to $Z=(L,R)$, whose independent Brownian coordinates have variance $1/2$ per unit time. The limiting contour has the peanosphere coupling with a $\sqrt2$-quantum cone $h$ and an independent whole-plane space-filling $\mathrm{SLE}_8$, subsequently parameterized by $\mu_h$. Write this curve as $\eta$ and normalize $\eta(0)=0$.*

3.  *The imaginary field $\widehat h$ is independent of $h$ and is a whole-plane GFF modulo $2\pi\chi\mathbb Z$, with uniform phase in one period. It determines the oriented curve before area parameterization. The fields, curve, and contour are retained with their joint peanosphere law.*

4.  *A finite interval of contour increments determines its abstract decorated quantum surface, its restricted curve, and the quantum lengths of its incoming and outgoing frontiers, modulo conformal coordinates. The analogous statement holds for every symmetric interior interval of the Brownian-excursion encoding of the ordinary unit-area sphere.*

</div>

The discrete assertion is Mullin’s encoding <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Mullin](#ref-Mullin)], in the form of Theorem “Mullin encoding and its infinite version” in <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]. The continuum assertions are recorded in its Theorem “Peanosphere and interval surfaces” and its imaginary-field normalization. Their principal continuum inputs are the mating of trees theorems of <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[DMS](#ref-DMS), Theorems 1.9 and 1.11] and the space-filling construction of <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[IG4](#ref-IG4)]. The use of a Poisson clock makes the two coordinate jump processes independent; index time divided by $m$ has the same limiting area time.

<a id="inp:brownian-fibers"></a>
We recall the geometric information accompanying this coupling. Two continuum times have the same image under $\eta$ precisely when they are equivalent under the relation generated by $$\label{inp:brownian-fibers}
 L_s=L_t=\inf_{[s,t]}L
 \quad\hbox{or}\quad
 R_s=R_t=\inf_{[s,t]}R,\qquad s\le t.$$ Except for the common endpoint in the sphere encoding, every class has at most three elements and belongs to one coordinate when it is nonsingleton. In a class $t_1<t_2<t_3$, the middle time is a strict local minimum; distinct strict local minima have different heights. At parameter $8$, the interval cells are closed topological disks. The cone curve is proper, so inverse images of compact sets are bounded. These facts, including their simultaneous versions on a countable collection of intervals and independent Poisson cuts, are the topological input accompanying Theorem “Peanosphere and interval surfaces” of <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]; see also the identification description in <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[DMS](#ref-DMS), Section 1.3.1 and Lemma 8.15].

All subsequential arguments below can be placed in a coupling with locally uniform contour convergence. We attach the full continuum background conditionally on the limiting contour, independently of the additional *raw* discrete observations given that contour. Here raw means that the observation uses contour labels, without projecting them through $\eta$. This convention is compatible with further extraction. Indeed, for a bounded background test $F$, its conditional expectation $k_F(Z)$ can be approximated in $L^1$ of the fixed Brownian marginal by bounded continuous functions of $Z$. Multiplying by any bounded raw test leaves the approximation error uniformly bounded by the same $L^1$ error. Joint weak convergence therefore preserves the conditional sampling identity. Spatially projected observations will depend on both the raw data and the background; we only make this conditional sampling assertion before projection.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Projections and planar topology

Regard the map together with its dual as a cell decomposition of a surface, subdivided into triangles at its corners. Assign a primal or dual vertex the point $\eta(t)$ corresponding to any of its visits. All choices at one vertex, and all corners of an incident triangle, have uniformly vanishing discrepancy. Otherwise a subsequence of visit times would limit to a discrete equality or matching relation whose endpoints have distinct continuum images, contradicting <a href="#inp:brownian-fibers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:brownian-fibers">[inp:brownian-fibers]</a>. Join nearby assigned points by short coordinate arcs and fill the triangles in small disks. This gives continuous projections $\pi_m$ with vanishing projected triangle mesh. On the plane this construction is made in a bounded window containing the region under consideration, after preserving its necessary stars and capping the window. This protection is obtained directly from lower contour visits, as in the proof below; it does not use a local instruction graph or any numerical-array limit.

<a id="stmt-inp-topology"></a>
<div id="inp:topology" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Topological comparison). *In the contour coupling, the projections $\pi_m$ may be chosen so that the following conclusions hold on the sphere and on every buffered compact part of the plane. Each conclusion is eventual for each fixed choice of margins.*

1.  *If the projections of two primal vertices approach each other, they can be joined by primal paths with arbitrarily small projected diameter. This is uniform over all lifts in a fixed compact region. The same statement holds on the dual graph and for surface paths. In particular fixed tubes with clearance contain paths following them on either graph.*

2.  *All lifts of a sufficiently small closed disk or other compact well can be included in a connected set over any fixed open enlargement. A loop projecting into a small disk can be filled over a slightly larger disk, provided that both have the prescribed clearance.*

3.  *The projection has degree $+1$ or $-1$ in a compact spherical completion. Robustly transverse primal and dual tube crossings intersect at an edge and its dual. Projected circuits with nonzero winding difference about two separated wells separate all the corresponding lifted fibers.*

4.  *Integer winding differences about two wells agree upstairs and downstairs up to the common degree sign, when loops and wells have fixed separation. These statements retain repeated edge and face incidences.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inp-topology"><summary>Proof of stmt-inp-topology</summary>

*Proof.* The construction and the first three assertions are the construction preceding Lemma “Uniform topological joining”, that lemma, Lemma “Exterior connection and small-loop filling”, Proposition “Degree and robust planar topology”, and Lemma “Separation of entire fibers” in <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]. We explain their applicability to both graphs and to the plane.

The hypotheses are uniform contour convergence, vanishing projected cell mesh, and approximation of the limiting fiber generators by discrete links with small projected connecting paths. For a Brownian excursion above a level, use last and first crossings of nearby levels on the two sides of an interior time. Their discrete analogues approximate the chord. At a triple, apply this on the two excursions on either side of its middle minimum. The fiber description above therefore supplies every generator required for topological joining. Interchanging the coordinates gives the same argument for the dual. Applying planar separation to the two embedded graph paths gives the mixed crossing assertion: a primal path and a dual path can meet only through a primal edge and its dual, with the usual corner subdivision making the intersection literal on the surface.

For a bounded plane region, choose a window containing all its visits and its fixed additional star layers. Both contour coordinates make strictly lower visits on both sides of a sufficiently large window. Lemma “Protected capping of a bounded window” in <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)] then supplies a spherical completion preserving all these incidences. Its continuum completion is absolutely continuous with respect to a Brownian excursion, which supplies the sphere quotient needed for the preceding topological results.

For the integer assertion, connect all lifts of each well away from the tested loop, using the first two assertions. A two-chain bounded by that loop has constant multiplicity along each such connected fiber. Projection multiplies the difference of its multiplicities at the two fibers by $\deg\pi_m=\pm1$. This is exactly the equality of winding differences. Equivalently, one can use relative homology after a simplicial perturbation inside the fixed margins. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Finite instruction graphs and numerical tests

<a id="inp:graph-energy"></a>
For a finite multigraph $G$, write $$\label{inp:graph-energy}
 \mathsf e_G(u)=\sum_{ab\in E(G)}(u(a)-u(b))^2.$$ The sum has one term for each unoriented edge; loops contribute zero. An energy test is the infimum of <a href="#inp:graph-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:graph-energy">[inp:graph-energy]</a> subject to finitely specified value constraints at vertices of a specified subgraph. We allow $+\infty$ for an infeasible test. An extremal-length test for a family $\mathcal P$ of graph paths is <a id="inp:el-test"></a>$$\label{inp:el-test}
 \mathop{\mathrm{EL}}_G(\mathcal P)=
 \sup_{w:E(G)\to[0,\infty)}
 \frac{\bigl(\inf_{P\in\mathcal P}\sum_{e\in P}w(e)\bigr)^2}
      {\sum_{e\in E(G)}w(e)^2},$$ with the usual conventions for empty families and constant paths. Only nontrivial families will be used in the crossing arguments.

The graph on which one computes these quantities must be specified before taking a limit. We use the finite instruction graphs of Lemma “Finite-band reconstruction” in <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)], recalled next. Their inputs consist of finite strings and relative heights. This description will also show why the conditional-law proof applies to our numerical tests.

<a id="stmt-inp-packages"></a>
<div id="inp:packages" class="definition">

**Definition 4** (Finite packages). A *master block* consists of two increment strings on a time interval $[0,\ell_b]$, starting at zero, together with a finite list of internal cuts. A finite package contains finitely many master blocks. For each coordinate $a\in\{L,R\}$ choose a forest on these blocks and record its height differences $o^a_{bd}=s^a_d-s^a_b$. The values $s^a_b$ are thereby determined within each forest component up to one additive constant.

A test chooses finitely many *active windows*, each between recorded cuts of a master block. Each active window initially has its own copy of the contour vertices. Its starting heights and increment strings are obtained by restriction of its master block. Enable the ordinary sewing inside each active window. The additional cross-window links are specified by the band rules below. A package can retain several tests on the same master strings.

</div>

<a id="inp:bands"></a>
For an active window $b$, write $z^a_b$ for its increment string, $s^a_b$ for its starting height, and $m^a_b=\min z^a_b$. Its incoming and outgoing record arcs are the times satisfying, respectively, $$z^a_b(t)=\min_{0\le u\le t}z^a_b(u),
 \qquad
 z^a_b(t)=\min_{t\le u\le\ell_b}z^a_b(u).$$ A band from window $b$ to window $d$ permits outgoing-to-incoming links with common absolute height $H$ satisfying $$\label{inp:bands}
 \alpha\le H-q\le\beta,
 \qquad H\le s^a_w+\min_J z^a_w\quad(w,J\text{ in the cap list}).$$ Here $\alpha<\beta$ are fixed rational parameters, $q$ is the height at a recorded anchor cut, and each $J$ is a recorded subwindow. The forest supplies every relative height appearing in the rule. Each selected minimum is evaluated from the discrete string itself.

In the primal graph, an $L$-band identifies its permitted vertices. An $R$-band is a rule for pairs of oriented step occurrences: an outgoing up-step and an incoming down-step must carry the same unit height strip, and the *whole strip* must satisfy the band and cap conditions. Such a pair contributes an edge; it does not impose an $R$-vertex identification. The dual uses the exchanged convention. If a tree edge is represented in several retained pieces it is counted once after the corresponding vertex identifications: its unordered endpoint pair identifies it, since a tree has no parallel edges. Each additional matched-step edge is counted once for that matched pair, even when another such pair has the same endpoints.

The same active windows and band instructions continue to define an old test when cuts are added or master blocks are merged. In the merged string, old starting heights are evaluated at the old cuts; they are no longer independent offset parameters. A graph allowed to use the entire merged interval is a separate new test. In particular an old test cannot be recovered by merely confining paths in that larger graph, since the larger graph may use newly enabled edges witnessed in a formerly omitted gap.

For each instruction list we retain a countable family of numerical outputs. A vertex is selected when one of its visits lies in a finite union of intervals of relative time. Endpoints can be recorded cuts or rational fractions between cuts. Such selectors specify the subgraph, its ports, and any confinement condition. Rational value intervals at selected visits specify energy constraints; repeated visits impose simultaneous constraints on the same vertex. The output library includes both graphs, finite joint lists of tests, and tests recomputed on union graphs. Inapplicable tests receive a fixed default value. Every nonnegative output is compactified in $[0,\infty]$, for example by $x\mapsto x/(1+x)$. We can retain outputs divided by any prescribed positive deterministic factor $b_m$, including factors tending to zero.

This gives compact countable products of numerical observations. The master strings, durations, cuts, and offsets remain separate input coordinates. Along any sequence $m\to\infty$, one may therefore extract joint limits of all the raw tests and the locally convergent contours. The issue is now to relate these formal tests to complete pieces of the actual map.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Exact cores and the selector sandwich

Add independent Poisson cuts in area time, using nested refinements. A cell is the image of a consecutive cut interval under $\eta$. Only cells compactly contained in the current testing domain will be used. Their local data consist of their oriented traversals, quantum area parameterizations, and tagged incoming and outgoing frontier lengths, with repeated frontier occurrences retained. A *run* is a finite union of consecutive cells, viewed with its concatenated traversal and tagged incoming and outgoing frontiers. For any finite selection of cells, its maximal runs are the maximal consecutive groups in that selection.

Here is how these local data provide the relative heights used in a package. Fix a run $b$ and one coordinate $a\in\{L,R\}$. For an initial segment of the run, the incoming frontier length is the drop from its starting contour height to the minimum on that segment; the outgoing length is the rise from that minimum to its ending height. Their difference is therefore the contour increment. Reading these two tagged lengths at every rational relative area time, and using continuity, recovers the whole increment path $z_b^a$ from the local traversal. Put $m_b^a=\min z_b^a$ and $e_b^a=z_b^a(\ell_b)$.

The incoming frontier of the whole run has relative-height range $[m_b^a,0]$, and the outgoing frontier has range $[m_b^a,e_b^a]$. For a tagged point $p$ on either frontier, define its *relative height* $\vartheta_b^a(p)$ by starting with value $m_b^a$ at the minimum endpoint and increasing by quantum boundary length along that frontier toward its tip. Thus the incoming tip has height $0$ and the outgoing tip has height $e_b^a$. This orientation is by increasing height, irrespective of the orientation in which the contour traverses the frontier. Equivalently, if $p$ is represented by a record time $t$, then $\vartheta_b^a(p)=z_b^a(t)$.

<a id="inp:seam-offset"></a>
A *directed shared seam* $b\to d$ is a common frontier subarc of positive quantum length, tagged as outgoing for $b$ and incoming for $d$, in the same coordinate. At a paired point $p$ of this seam, the two absolute heights agree. Hence $$\label{inp:seam-offset}
 s_b^a+\vartheta_b^a(p)=s_d^a+\vartheta_d^a(p),
 \qquad
 s_d^a-s_b^a=\vartheta_b^a(p)-\vartheta_d^a(p).$$ The right side is constant along the seam and is determined by the two local tagged frontier parameterizations. One may read it at an interior point, so the construction does not require selecting or assigning a height to a branch endpoint. Taking a spanning forest of such seams supplies exactly the offsets in Definition <a href="#inp:packages" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inp:packages">4</a>. Their outgoing and incoming tags also specify which linked run precedes the other. A chronological order between runs without a declared seam is unnecessary.

<a id="inp:exact-cap"></a>
There is one endpoint configuration requiring an exact comparison. Suppose three visits of one coordinate meet at height $H$, in three disjoint runs $b,w,d$ in that order, with the middle visit a strict local minimum. The outer runs $b,d$ share a positive interval of seam heights below $H$. Above $H$, the two positive seam intervals are $b\to w$ and $w\to d$; see Figure <a href="#inp:triple-figure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inp:triple-figure">1</a>. Formula <a href="#inp:seam-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:seam-offset">[inp:seam-offset]</a> on these intervals recovers $s_w^a$ relative to the outer starts. The tag directions identify $w$ as the middle run. It lies between the two outer visits and contains the middle contact. The contour stays at least $H$ throughout that interval by <a href="#inp:brownian-fibers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:brownian-fibers">[inp:brownian-fibers]</a>, and attains $H$ in $w$. Hence $$\label{inp:exact-cap}
                       H=s_w^a+m_w^a.$$ A band for the outer pair may extend above the continuum value $H$, but it must include the cap in <a href="#inp:bands" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:bands">[inp:bands]</a> evaluated as $s_{w,m}^a+\min z_{w,m}^a$ at every discrete resolution. It is this exact minimum, rather than a rounded approximation of $H$, that excludes the outer-to-outer links above the branch.

<figure id="inp:triple-figure">

<figcaption>A triple contact in height coordinates. The shaded strips indicate positive intervals of shared quantum-boundary heights; they are not drawings of Euclidean seams. The outer pair is linked below $H$, while above $H$ each outer run is linked to the middle run. The middle run’s exact discrete minimum caps the outer-pair band.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-inp-triple-figure"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`inp:triple-figure`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.85cm,y=0.9cm,>=Stealth,
                    every node/.style={font=\small}]
 \fill[blue!7] (-0.15,2.1) rectangle (6.15,2.55);
 \fill[blue!7] (-0.15,0.4) rectangle (6.15,0.85);
 \draw[->,gray] (-1.7,0) -- (-1.7,3.1) node[above] {height};
 \draw[thick] (0,0.15) -- (0,2.95) node[above] {$b$};
 \draw[thick] (3,1.5) -- (3,2.95) node[above] {$w$};
 \draw[thick] (6,0.15) -- (6,2.95) node[above] {$d$};
 \draw[->,blue!65!black,thick] (0.08,2.3) -- (2.92,2.3);
 \draw[->,blue!65!black,thick] (3.08,2.3) -- (5.92,2.3);
 \draw[->,blue!65!black,thick] (0.08,0.62) -- (5.92,0.62);
 \node[above] at (1.5,2.3) {$b\to w$};
 \node[above] at (4.5,2.3) {$w\to d$};
 \node[below] at (3,0.62) {$b\to d$};
 \draw[densely dashed] (-1.9,1.5) -- (6.4,1.5);
 \node[left] at (-1.9,1.5) {$H$};
 \fill (3,1.5) circle (2pt);
 \node[anchor=west,fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (3.25,1.73)
            {$s_w^a+m_w^a$};
 \draw[red!65!black,very thick] (-0.65,0.45) -- (-0.65,2.5);
 \draw[red!65!black] (-0.85,0.45) -- (-0.45,0.45);
 \draw[red!65!black] (-0.85,2.5) -- (-0.45,2.5);
 \node[anchor=east,rotate=90,red!65!black] at (-0.93,2.45)
            {uncapped band};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 12 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A schematic in height coordinates. A vertical axis on the left is labeled "height", with a horizontal dashed line at level $H$. Three vertical lines are labeled $b$, $w$, and $d$ from left to right. A vertical shaded reddish strip to the left of line $b$ is labeled vertically as "uncapped band". Three horizontal directed arrows show connections between runs: an arrow $b \to w$ above $H$, an arrow $w \to d$ above $H$, and a blue arrow $b \to d$ below level $H$. On line $w$ at height $H$, a marked level is labeled $s_w^a + m_w^a$.
```


We use bands lying inside these shared height intervals with positive margins at their effective endpoints, with two exceptions prescribed by the record data. At a natural lower record minimum the rational band starts below the minimum and is clipped by the exact record condition. At an upper endpoint with the three-seam configuration, the band is clipped by the exact selected minimum <a href="#inp:exact-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:exact-cap">[inp:exact-cap]</a>. These are the admissible band choices in the proof of Lemma “Finite-band reconstruction” of <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]. All their tests use the local tagged frontiers, relative increments, and quantum lengths just described.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-inp-complete-core"></a>
<div id="inp:complete-core" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Complete graph pieces in a buffer). *Fix $K\Subset U$ in a coordinate chart. Almost surely, there is a finite prescription, selectable from the local cells in $U$ and their frontier data, with the following property. In a neighborhood of $K$, its instruction graph agrees with the actual discrete graph, including vertex equalities and all edge incidences, for all sufficiently large resolutions in the coupling. The same prescription can retain both the primal and the dual graph. One can require any fixed smaller collar of this neighborhood before restricting the tests to it.*

*The prescriptions can be taken from the countable instruction scheme above and chosen measurably using the fields and spatial cut marks in $U$, with an arbitrarily small open buffer. The assertion also holds in the interior of a fixed interval cell, using only its internal increment string.*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inp-complete-core"><summary>Proof of stmt-inp-complete-core</summary>

*Proof.* We use the exact-incidence part of the proof of Lemma “Finite-band reconstruction” in <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]. We give the argument because exactness, rather than an approximation of path costs, is essential for energy tests.

Choose $r>0$ so that the closed $3r$-neighborhood of $K$ lies in $U$. Properness bounds all its visit times in a finite window. Refine the Poisson cuts until every cell meeting this neighborhood has diameter less than $r$. Select all cells meeting the closed $2r$-neighborhood of $K$ and merge consecutive selected cells into maximal runs. These runs are disjoint intervals; all visits sufficiently near $K$ lie in them. Their endpoints stay away from $K$, since otherwise the neighboring cell would also have been selected. Local traversal data identify the selection: first inspect a slightly larger buffer and require the portions around every visit to the smaller compact to be flanked by marks while they remain in that buffer. Properness, continuity, and refinement make this condition hold eventually. No global index for the retained runs is part of this inspection.

Consider one coordinate and two selected runs $b<d$ in chronological order. An outgoing point of $b$ and an incoming point of $d$ at height $H$ are linked exactly when <a id="inp:gap-test"></a>$$\label{inp:gap-test}
 H\le\min_{\text{between the two runs}} Z^a.$$ The same assertion holds discretely, with the full-strip convention for a matched step. Split the intervening interval into selected runs and omitted gaps. If a limiting link projects near $K$, any intervening time at its height represents that same continuum point. It must therefore belong to a selected cell. Thus every omitted-gap minimum is strictly above the height of that link. The relevant endpoint pairs form a compact set, and there are only finitely many gaps. Their positive slack is consequently uniform: a sequence of slacks tending to zero would limit to a contact in an omitted gap, contrary to the selection.

An obstruction without slack can only be a minimum of a selected run. At a triple contact, the three directed seams in Figure <a href="#inp:triple-figure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inp:triple-figure">1</a> identify the middle run and recover its offset through <a href="#inp:seam-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:seam-offset">[inp:seam-offset]</a>. Formula <a href="#inp:exact-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:exact-cap">[inp:exact-cap]</a>, evaluated on the discrete string, supplies the cap in <a href="#inp:bands" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:bands">[inp:bands]</a>. At a natural lower seam endpoint, choose the lower rational band endpoint below the record minimum; the exact incoming or outgoing record condition then clips the band at that minimum. This retains links at the minimum as well as links at nearby lattice heights.

For every pair of runs, cover the compact set of relevant link heights by finitely many such bands. Choose each band to be admissible over its entire effective height interval: that interval lies in a genuine shared seam, its omitted-gap comparisons have positive slack, and every allowed non-strict cap is the exact selected minimum just described. The natural record conditions provide the exact clipping at lower seam endpoints. Include the positive seam intervals needed to read the relative heights, and retain a spanning forest of these comparisons in each coordinate. All needed offsets are sums along that forest. Seam directions specify the order of linked runs and distinguish each middle cap; the instructions do not need an order between unrelated runs. Each band retains every discrete link satisfying its rule. Uniform contour convergence, the positive omitted-gap slack, and the exact selected minima show two things: every actual link near $K$ belongs to one of these bands eventually, and every permitted link in those effective bands is an actual link eventually. In particular the latter assertion applies to any permitted link with one endpoint over a smaller core collar: its two limiting endpoints lie on the same shared-seam point and hence remain inside the captured collar. The assertions hold simultaneously for all links, regardless of their number.

Vertex equality is the same first-coordinate minimum test, so it is captured as well. Start a chain of enabled identifications at a core representative. Its first link is an actual identification by the preceding band validity. All visits to that actual vertex have uniformly close projections, independently of the instruction graph, and lie in the captured collar. Inductively the next enabled link is therefore actual as well and remains at that vertex. Thus a peripheral chain cannot introduce an additional equality at the core. Horizontal tree traversals with the same identified endpoint pair give one tree edge; no two distinct tree edges have that pair. Added edges are instead identified by their matched step-occurrence pair, preserving distinct parallel edges. Hence the result is equality of graphs with incidences, not merely equality of their path relations. Applying the argument to the other coordinate gives the dual conclusion.

All seam, cap, and positive-margin conditions are measurable in the oriented local cells and frontier lengths. Rational bands, integer lower bounds on positive margins, and finite forest choices give a countable selection rule. Taking a further collar before the final restriction gives the stated neighborhood of $K$. Inside a fixed interval cell, every visit to a compact subset of its interior lies in that interval with a time margin; the same argument then uses only its internal string. ◻

</details>

An arbitrary union of overlapping formal window copies need not be an injective copy of a map subgraph. The lemma asserts the existence of *complete* prescriptions, obtained from disjoint maximal runs or from a freshly computed merged graph. All comparisons to the actual map below use these complete prescriptions.

<a id="stmt-inp-sandwich"></a>
<div id="inp:sandwich" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Spatial and value selectors). *In the setting of Lemma <a href="#inp:complete-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inp:complete-core">5</a>, suppose $K\subset O$ with $K$ compact, $O$ open, and $\overline O\Subset U$. There is an interval selector on a complete prescription which, eventually, includes every vertex projected into $K$ and includes only vertices projected into $O$. Several such choices may be made together, for confinement and for each port set.*

*Let $u$ be continuous on a neighborhood of $\overline O$. For any two uniform value tolerances $0<\delta_-<\delta_+$, finite rational interval constraints on selected visit labels can be placed between the constraints $|v-u\circ\pi_m|\le\delta_-$ on a slightly larger compact set and $|v-u\circ\pi_m|\le\delta_+$ on a smaller one. All these comparisons hold simultaneously for every continuous target through the same retained test library.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inp-sandwich"><summary>Proof of stmt-inp-sandwich</summary>

*Proof.* On the finitely many selected windows, the limiting parameterizations are continuous. Cover the inverse image of $K$ by finitely many relative-time intervals with closures projecting inside $O$. Their endpoints may be chosen from the prescribed dense countable relative-time library. Uniform projection accuracy and the complete collar of Lemma <a href="#inp:complete-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inp:complete-core">5</a> give the desired inclusions. Select a vertex when any of its visits is in this union. The same argument applies to each port, while vanishing edge mesh allows an induced-edge rule within the complete prescription.

For value constraints, refine the intervals until the oscillation of $u\circ\eta$ on each is less than a fixed fraction of $\delta_+-\delta_-$. Choose a rational value interval between the two allowed tolerances. Impose it at every selected visit in that interval, retaining all simultaneous constraints when a vertex has several visits. The strict tolerance gap and uniform projection error give the two implications. The construction chooses among an already countable collection of tests; it does not add a new exceptional null set for each target $u$. Compact exhaustion and rational positive tolerances therefore yield common measurable versions for all continuous targets. ◻

</details>

The selector lemma compares variational values with strict margins. It does not assert convergence for a prescribed sharp boundary. For a crossing event, for example, choose smaller port and confinement constraints and then relaxed constraints, both strictly inside the desired geometry. An intermediate selector produces actual paths in the relaxed test, and any path family having the smaller clearance can be included by a sufficiently relaxed selector. Thus existence of a strictly fitting test with finite limiting extremal length is independent of the particular complete prescription. Exhausted energy values have the same property. These conventions also make readouts with a varying spatial center jointly measurable: all membership and value comparisons use finite selectors with a strict gap, followed by countable exhaustion.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The conditional law of enlarged outputs

We now identify the conditional law of the finite numerical arrays. The proof is the finite-experiment argument of <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)], with the output vector enlarged before taking a subsequence. The enlargement is harmless for a specific reason: every change of density in that argument is a function of the inputs alone.

<a id="stmt-inp-kernel-transfer"></a>
<div id="inp:kernel-transfer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Conditional transfer for numerical outputs). *Consider finitely many packages as in Definition <a href="#inp:packages" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inp:packages">4</a>. Different packages use disjoint master blocks and disjoint offset forests. Sample their Poissonized strings independently; give their durations, cuts, and offset vectors smooth positive densities, with offsets discretized on the height lattice. Retain any finite or countable vector of compactified numerical tests of their instruction graphs, including tests on both graphs.*

<a id="inp:conditional-product"></a>
*Along a common subsequence the reference laws have disintegrations $$\nu_r(\mathrm dx_r)K_r(x_r,\mathrm dy_r),$$ where $x_r$ denotes the durations, cuts, increment strings, and forest offsets, and $y_r$ denotes the numerical outputs. Insert these blocks in the actual bilateral walk with positive omitted gaps. Conditional on the entire limiting contour and its cuts, their output law is $$\label{inp:conditional-product}
             \bigotimes_r K_r(x_r,\mathrm dy_r).$$ The kernels are invariant under changes of positive reference densities and under relabeling blocks or changing the forest basis. They are compatible with jointly retained refinements and merged tests. These identities hold after selection from the countable instruction library using the limiting contour and cuts.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inp-kernel-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-inp-kernel-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* This is the argument of Lemma “Finite reference experiment and conditional transfer” in <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[MetricMaps](#ref-MetricMaps)]. We include its measure calculation to specify its application to potentially discontinuous variational outputs.

<a id="inp:density"></a>
Write $\epsilon_m=m^{-1/2}$. For one coordinate, order all selected blocks chronologically as $1,\ldots,k$. Let $s_i$ be their starts, $e_i$ their net increments, and $g_i>0$ the intervening durations. Complete the union of the package forests to a tree $T$ on these blocks. Give its extra offsets independent positive densities. If the density on an edge $e$ is $\rho_e$, use lattice mass $$r_{m,e}(a)=\int_{a-\epsilon_m/2}^{a+\epsilon_m/2}
                      \rho_e(u)\,\mathrm du,
 \qquad a\in\epsilon_m\mathbb Z.$$ Let $p_t(j)$ be the transition mass of one coordinate of the unscaled rate-one axial Poisson walk, so its variance rate is $\sigma^2=1/2$. The exact density of the actual relative starts with respect to these reference offsets is $$\label{inp:density}
 W_m^a=
 \frac{\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}
       p_{mg_i}\bigl(\epsilon_m^{-1}(s_{i+1}-s_i-e_i)\bigr)}
      {\displaystyle\prod_{e\in T}r_{m,e}(a_e)}.$$ Indeed, tree differences and consecutive differences are related by an integer linear map with integer inverse, obtained by summing along paths in $T$. Its determinant is $\pm1$, so there is no lattice multiplicity. Multiply <a href="#inp:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:density">[inp:density]</a> for the two coordinates and include the density ratio for the durations and cuts to obtain $W_m$.

On compact input sets with all gaps bounded below by a positive constant, the local central limit theorem gives $$\epsilon_m^{-1}p_{mt}(j)
 \longrightarrow
 (2\pi\sigma^2t)^{-1/2}
       \exp\{-x^2/(2\sigma^2t)\},
 \qquad x=\epsilon_m j,\quad \sigma^2=\tfrac12,$$ uniformly in bounded $x$ and in such $t$. The numerator and denominator of <a href="#inp:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:density">[inp:density]</a> have the same number of lattice factors. They cancel, leaving uniform convergence $W_m\to W$ to a continuous finite function on these compact sets.

Conditional on the selected inputs, the omitted strings are independent bridges and are independent of the numerical outputs. Their rescaled laws converge uniformly on these input sets to Brownian bridge laws. For completeness, their finite-dimensional densities are ratios of transition masses, so the preceding local limit theorem applies. On the first half of a bridge the density relative to the free walk is bounded by $$\frac{\sup_j p_{m(t-u)}(j)}{p_{mt}(j_0)}\le C,
 \qquad u\le t/2,$$ when $t$ and $\epsilon_mj_0$ range in the chosen compact sets. The numerator is at most $C\epsilon_m$ and the denominator at least $c\epsilon_m$. Free-walk tightness and the reversed argument on the other half prove uniform bridge tightness. The same applies to a finite list of gaps and to exterior contour portions on a bounded window.

<a id="inp:weighted-law"></a>
The full independent reference experiments converge jointly to the product of their limits, because their output spaces are compact and their input marginals converge. Let $a$ denote the completion offsets and $z$ the omitted bridges. Integrating bounded continuous bridge tests first, the preceding uniform convergence gives the limiting actual law $$\label{inp:weighted-law}
 W(x,a)\,\nu(\mathrm dx,\mathrm da)\,
       \mathcal B(x,a;\mathrm dz)\prod_rK_r(x_r,\mathrm dy_r).$$ The weight and the bridge law depend only on contour inputs. The complete contour and cuts recover $(x,a,z)$, so disintegration of <a href="#inp:weighted-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:weighted-law">[inp:weighted-law]</a> gives <a href="#inp:conditional-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inp:conditional-product">[inp:conditional-product]</a>.

To remove the compact restrictions, first keep all finitely many limiting gaps positive, then bound endpoints and offsets, and finally restrict the block paths to compact sets. Under the actual law the excluded probability tends to zero by finite-window contour tightness. If $P_m$ is the actual law and $Q_m$ the reference law, the exact identity $$\mathbb E_{Q_m}[W_m\mathbf 1_{A^c}]=P_m(A^c)$$ controls the omitted density mass. No global bound on $W_m$ is required. Exhaustion, followed by a monotone-class argument, gives the assertion for bounded measurable tests and for the entire bilateral contour.

A change of reference density adds another input-only density ratio, continuous in the limit on compact sets, and thus leaves $K_r$ unchanged. Forest bases are related by unimodular integer maps. The pushed-forward lattice cells and the directly discretized cells have the same volume and vanishing diameter. Their mass ratios converge to one on compact sets; tightness makes the two lattice laws converge in total variation. This comparison remains valid after applying a discontinuous graph-output map. Absolute time labels and the order of unrelated blocks occur only in $W_m$, and disappear from the conditional output law.
